Proof.

Immediately from Lemmas
25
and
26
.
∎

Remark 28
.

Observe that Lemma
27
would also hold with the rules
\mathsf{w_{\forall}}
and
\mathsf{c_{\forall}}
removed.

VII-C

Rule Permutations

Theorem 29
.

Let
\Gamma
be a sequent. If
\vdash\Gamma
is provable in
\mathsf{LK1}
(as depicted on the left below) then there is a sequent

\Gamma^{\prime}
not containing any
{\mathsf{f}}
, such that there is a derivation as shown on the right below:

{{{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}}\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 12.1528pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\quad$}}\kern 10.00002pt}\hbox{\hbox{$$}}\kern 10.00002pt}\hbox{\hbox{$$}}}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{LK1}\kern 2.5pt$}}\vbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}{}}{{}{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 8.18985pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle}$}}}}}}}}}}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\kern 2.5pt\scriptstyle\Phi$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{$$}}\kern 15.00002pt}}}\kern 5.70836pt}}}\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 16.52785pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$$}}\vbox{}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$$}\hss}\kern 10.0834pt}\hbox{\kern 16.52785pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vdash\Gamma\phantom{\vdash}$}}\kern 10.0834pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{{{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}}}}}\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 21.04172pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\quad$}}\kern 10.00002pt}\hbox{\hbox{$$}}\kern 10.00002pt}\hbox{\hbox{$$}}}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{MLL1^{X}}\kern 2.5pt$}}\vbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}{}}{{}{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 8.18985pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle}$}}}}}}}}}}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\kern 2.5pt\scriptstyle\Phi_{1}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{$$}}\kern 15.00002pt}}}\kern 9.61119pt}}}\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 17.34726pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$$}}\vbox{}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$$}\hss}\kern 5.91673pt}\hbox{\kern 17.34726pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vdash\bigvee\mkern-1.0mu(\mkern-2.0mu\Gamma^{\prime}\mkern-2.0mu)\phantom{\vdash}$}}\kern 5.91673pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 17.34726pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\vbox{\hbox{\hfil}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\hss}\kern 5.91673pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 36.04173pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.20001pt$}}\kern 24.6112pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern-1.43518pt\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 36.04173pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{c},\equiv\}\;$}}\vbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{$\vbox to12.0pt{\hbox{\vrule width=0.6pt,height=12.0pt\kern 1.2pt\vrule width=0.6pt,height=12.0pt}}$}}}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\;\Phi_{2}$}\hss}\kern 24.6112pt}\kern-1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 36.04173pt\hbox{\kern 1.20001pt}\kern 24.6112pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 18.75003pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\vbox{\hbox{\hfil}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\hss}\kern 7.3195pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 18.75003pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vdash\bigvee\mkern-1.0mu(\mkern-2.0mu\Gamma\mkern-2.0mu)\phantom{\vdash}$}}\kern 7.3195pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}

Proof.

First, we can replace every instance of the
{\mathsf{f}}
-rule in
\Phi
by

\mathsf{wk}
. Then the instances of
\mathsf{wk}
and
\mathsf{ctr}
are replaced by

\mathsf{w}
and
\mathsf{c}
, which can then be permuted down.
Details are in
Appendix
-A
.
∎

Lemma 30
.

For every derivation

{\mathsf{t}}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{MLS1^{X}}\;

A

there are formulas
A^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime\prime}
such that

{\mathsf{t}}

\scriptstyle\{\forall,\mathsf{ai},{\mathsf{t}}\}\;

A^{\prime\prime}

\scriptstyle\{{\mathsf{s}},\mathsf{mix},\equiv\}\;

A^{\prime}

\scriptstyle\{\exists\}\;

A

Proof.

First, observe that the
\exists
rule can be permuted under all the
other rules since
A[x/t]
has the same structure as
A
and
none of the other rules has a premise of the form
S\{\exists x.A\}
. It suffices now to prove that all rules in
\{\forall,\mathsf{ai},{\mathsf{t}}\}
can be permuted over the rules in
\{{\mathsf{s}},\mathsf{mix},\equiv\}
, which is straightforward. For
{\mathsf{s}}
,
\mathsf{mix}
, and the
\equiv
-instances that
do not involve the quantifiers, the details can be found
in
[
38
]
. The
\equiv
-instances concerning the
quantifiers are admissible if the
\exists
-rule
is not present.
∎

Lemma 31
.

For every derivation

A

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}},{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}},\equiv\}\;

B

there are formulas
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
such that

A

\scriptstyle\{{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}},\equiv\}\;

A^{\prime}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}}\}\;

B^{\prime}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\equiv\}\;

B

Proof.

Permute all
\mathsf{w}
and
\mathsf{w_{\forall}}
instances to the bottom of
the derivation, then permute all
\mathsf{c}
and

\mathsf{c_{\forall}}
below
\{{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}}\}
. This involves a tedious but
routine case analysis. However, unlike most other rule
permutations in this paper, this has not been done before in the deep
inference literature.
For this reason, we give the full case
analysis in Appendix
-B
.
This
Lemma is the reason for the presence of the rules
\mathsf{w_{\forall}}
and

\mathsf{c_{\forall}}
, as without them the permutation cases in (
5
)
could not be resolved.
∎

We can now complete the proof of Theorems
17
and
18
.

Proof of Theorem
18
.

Assume we have a proof of
A
in
\mathsf{KS1}
. By
Theorem
24
we have a proof of
\vdash A
in
\mathsf{LK1}
to
which we can apply Theorem
29
. Finally, we
apply Lemma
20
to get the desired shape.
∎

Proof of Theorem
17
.

Assume we have a proof of
A
in
\mathsf{KS1}
. We first apply
Theorem
18
, and then
Lemma
30
to the upper half and
Lemmas
27
and
31
to the lower half.
∎

VIII
Fonets and Linear Proofs

VIII-A

From
\mathsf{MLL1^{X}}
Proofs to Fonets

Let
\Pi
be a
\mathsf{MLL1^{X}}
proof of a rectified sequent
\vdash\Gamma
not containing
{\mathsf{f}}
. We now show
how
\Pi
is translated into a linked fograph

\llbracket\Pi\rrbracket=\langle\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\sim_{\Pi}\rangle
. We proceed
inductively, making a case analysis on the last rule in
\Pi
. At the
same time we are constructing a dualizer
\delta_{\Pi}
, so that in the
end we can conclude that
\llbracket\Pi\rrbracket
is in fact a fonet.

1.

\Pi
is

\mathsf{ax}\;

\vdash a,\overline{a}

: Then the only
link is
\{a,\overline{a}\}
, and
\delta_{\Pi}
is empty.

2.

\Pi
is

{\mathsf{t}}\;

\vdash{\mathsf{t}}

: Then

\sim_{\Pi}
and
\delta_{\Pi}
are both empty.

3.

The last rule in
\Pi
is

\vdash\Gamma^{\prime}

\vdash\Gamma^{\prime\prime}

\mathsf{mix}\;

\vdash\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}

:
By induction hypothesis, we have proofs
\Pi^{\prime}
and
\Pi^{\prime\prime}
of
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
, respectively. We have

\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket=\llbracket\Gamma^{\prime}\rrbracket+\llbracket\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket
and we can let

\sim_{\Pi}\;=\;\sim_{\Pi^{\prime}}\cup\sim_{\Pi^{\prime\prime}}
and

\delta_{\Pi}=\delta_{\Pi^{\prime}}\cup\delta_{\Pi^{\prime\prime}}
.

4.

The last rule in
\Pi
is

\vdash\Gamma_{1},A,B

\mathbin{\vee}\;

\vdash\Gamma_{1},A\mathbin{\vee}B

: By induction hypothesis, there is
a proof
\Pi^{\prime}
of
\Gamma^{\prime}={\Gamma_{1},A,B}
. We have

\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket=\llbracket\Gamma^{\prime}\rrbracket
and let

\sim_{\Pi}\;=\;\sim_{\Pi^{\prime}}
and

\delta_{\Pi}=\delta_{\Pi^{\prime}}
.

5.

The last rule in
\Pi
is

\vdash\Gamma_{1},A

\vdash B,\Gamma_{2}

\mathbin{\wedge}\;

\vdash\Gamma_{1},A\mathbin{\wedge}B,\Gamma_{2}

: By induction
hypothesis, we have proofs
\Pi^{\prime}
and
\Pi^{\prime\prime}
of

\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma_{1},A
and
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}=B,\Gamma_{2}
, respectively. We
have
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket=\llbracket\Gamma_{1}\rrbracket+\mbox{$(\llbracket A\rrbracket\times\llbracket B\rrbracket)$}+\llbracket\Gamma_{2}\rrbracket
and we let

\sim_{\Pi}\;=\;\sim_{\Pi^{\prime}}\cup\sim_{\Pi^{\prime\prime}}
and

\delta_{\Pi}=\delta_{\Pi^{\prime}}\cup\delta_{\Pi^{\prime\prime}}
.

6.

The last rule in
\Pi
is

\vdash\Gamma_{1},A[x/t]

\exists\;

\vdash\Gamma_{1},\exists x.A

: By induction hypothesis, there is
a proof
\Pi^{\prime}
of
\Gamma^{\prime}={\Gamma_{1},A[x/t]}
. For each atom in

\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma_{1},A[x/t]
, there is a corresponding atom
in
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1},\exists x.A
. We can therefore define the
linking
\sim_{\Pi}
from the linking
\sim_{\Pi^{\prime}}
via
this correspondence. Then, we let
\delta_{\Pi}
be

\delta_{\Pi^{\prime}}+[x/t]
. Since
\Gamma
is rectified
x

does not yet occur in
\delta_{\Pi^{\prime}}
. Hence
\delta_{\Pi}
is a
dualizer of
\llbracket\Pi\rrbracket
.

7.

The last rule in
\Pi
is

\vdash\Gamma_{1},A

\forall\;

(
x
not free
in
\Gamma_{1}
)

\vdash\Gamma_{1},\forall x.A

:
By induction hypothesis, there is a proof
\Pi^{\prime}
of

\Gamma^{\prime}={\Gamma_{1},A}
, which has the same atoms as in

\Gamma=\Gamma_{1},\forall x.A
. Hence, we can let

\sim_{\Pi}\;=\;\sim_{\Pi^{\prime}}
and

\delta_{\Pi}=\delta_{\Pi^{\prime}}
.

Theorem 32
.

If
\Pi
is a
\mathsf{MLL1^{X}}
proof of a rectified
{\mathsf{f}}
-free sequent
\vdash\Gamma
,
then
\llbracket\Pi\rrbracket
is a fonet and
\delta_{\Pi}
a dualizer for it.

Proof.

We must show that none of the operations above introduces a
bimatching. For cases 1–6, this is immediate. For case 7, observe
that there is a potential dependency from each existential binder in

\llbracket\Gamma^{\prime}\rrbracket
to the new
x
-binder
\bullet x
in

\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket
. However, observe that this
\bullet x
vertex is
not connected to any vertex in
\llbracket\Gamma^{\prime}\rrbracket
, and hence no
such new dependency can be extended to a bimatching. That

\delta_{\Pi}
is a dualizer for
\llbracket\Pi\rrbracket
follows immediately
from the construction. Hence,
\llbracket\Pi\rrbracket
is a fonet.
∎

VIII-B

From
\mathsf{MLS1^{X}}
Proofs to Fonets

There is a more direct path from a
\mathsf{MLL1^{X}}
proof
\Pi
of a rectified
sequent
\Gamma
to the linked fograph
\llbracket\Pi\rrbracket
: take
the fograph
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket
, and let the equivalence classes of

\sim_{\Pi}
be all the atom pairs that meet in an instance of

\mathsf{ax}
, and
\delta_{\Pi}
comprises the substitutions
at the
\exists
-rules in
\Pi
.
We chose the more cumbersome path above because it gives us a
direct proof of Theorem
32
.
However, for translating
\mathsf{MLS1^{X}}
derivation into fonets, we employ
exactly that direct path.

In a derivation in
\mathsf{MLS1^{X}}

where the conclusion is rectified, every line is also rectified, as
the only rules involving bound variables are
\forall
and
\exists

which (upwards) both remove a binder.
Therefore, we can call such a derivation
rectified
, and
for a non-rectified
\mathsf{MLS1^{X}}
derivation
\Phi
we can
define its
rectification

\widehat{\Phi}
inductively, by rectifying each
line, proceeding step-wise
from conclusion to premise.
3
3

3

As for formulas,
the rectification of a derivation is unique up to renaming of bound
variables.

A rectified derivation

{\mathsf{t}}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{MLS1^{X}}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi

A

determines a
substitution which maps the existential bound variables occurring in

A
to the terms substituted for them in the instances of the

\exists
-rule in
\Phi
. We denote this substitution by

\delta_{\Phi}
and call it the
dualizer
of

\Phi
. Furthermore, every atom occurring in the conclusion
A
must
be consumed by a unique instance of the rule
\mathsf{ai}
in
\Phi
. This
allows us to define a (partial) equivalence relation
\sim_{\Phi}

on the atom occurrences in
A
by
a\sim_{\Phi}b
if
a
and
b

are consumed by the same instance of
\mathsf{ai}
in
\Phi
. We call

\sim_{\Phi}
the
linking
of
\Phi
, and define

\llbracket\Phi\rrbracket=\langle\llbracket A\rrbracket,\sim_{\Phi}\rangle
.

Theorem 33
.

Let

{\mathsf{t}}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{MLS1^{X}}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi

A

be a rectified derivation where
A
is
{\mathsf{f}}
-free. Then

\llbracket\Phi\rrbracket
is a fonet and
\delta_{\Phi}
a dualizer for it.

To prove this theorem, we have to show that no inference rule in

\mathsf{MLS1^{X}}
can introduce a bimatching. To simplify the argument, we
introduce the
frame

[
39
]
of the linked fograph
\mathcal{C}
,
which is a linked (propositional) cograph in which the dependencies
between the binders in
\mathcal{C}
are encoded as links.

More formally, let
C
be a formula with
\llbracket C\rrbracket=\mathcal{C}
, to which we
exhaustively apply the following
subformula rewriting steps, to obtain a sequent
C^{\star}
:

1.

Encode dependencies as fresh links.
For each dependency

\{\bullet x_{i},\bullet y_{j}\}
in
\mathcal{C}
, with corresponding
subformulas
\exists x_{i}.A
and
\forall y_{j}.B
in
C
, we pick a
fresh (nullary) predicate symbol
q_{i,j}
, and then replace
\exists x_{i}.A
by
\overline{q}_{i,j}\wedge\exists x_{i}.A
, and replace
\forall y_{j}.B
by

q_{i,j}\vee\forall y_{j}.B
.

2.

Erase quantifiers.
After step 1, remove all the
quantifiers, i.e., replace
\exists x_{i}.A
by
A
and replace
\forall y_{j}.B
by
B
everywhere.

3.

Simplify atoms.
After step 2, replace every predicate

pt_{1}\ldots t_{n}
(resp.
\overline{p}t_{1}\ldots t_{n}
) with a nullary
predicate symbol
p
(resp.
\overline{p}
)

Then
\sim_{C^{\star}}
consists of the pairs induced by

\sim_{\mathcal{C}}
and the new pairs
\{q_{i,j},\overline{q}_{i,j}\}
introduced in
step 1 above.
We call
C^{\star}
the
frame
of
C
and we define the
frame
of
\mathcal{C}
, denoted

\mathcal{C}^{\star}
, as

\langle\llbracket C^{\star}\rrbracket,\sim_{C^{\star}}\rangle
.

Lemma 34
.

If a linked fograph
\mathcal{C}
has an induced bimatching then so does its frame
\mathcal{C}^{\star}
.

Proof.

Immediately from the construction of the frame.
∎

Proof of Theorem
33
.

From
\Phi
we construct a derivation
\Phi^{\star}
of
A^{\star}
in the propositional fragment of
\mathsf{MLS1^{X}}
, such that

\llbracket\Phi^{\star}\rrbracket=\llbracket\Phi\rrbracket^{\star}
. The
rules
\mathsf{ai},{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{mix}
and
{\mathsf{s}}
are translated trivially, and for

\equiv
, it suffices to observe that the frame construction is
invariant under
\equiv
. Finally, for the rules
\forall
and

\exists
, proceed as follows. Every instance of
\forall
is
replaced by the derivation on the right below:
4
4

4

For better
readability we omit superfluous parentheses, knowing that we always
have
\equiv
incorporating associativity and commutativity of

\mathbin{\wedge}
and
\mathbin{\vee}
.

{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 8.33328pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 9.73756pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$S\{{\mathsf{t}}\}$}}}\kern 9.73756pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\forall\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to39.7946pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$S\{\forall y_{j}.{\mathsf{t}}\}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\;\leadsto{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}}}}\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 3.19444pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 56.69688pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 20.79997pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{${\mathsf{t}}$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\vbox{\hbox{\hfil}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 1.80556pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.20001pt$}}\kern 1.80556pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern-1.43518pt\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 1.80556pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{ai},{\mathsf{t}}\}\;$}}\vbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{$\vbox to12.0pt{\hbox{\vrule width=0.6pt,height=12.0pt\kern 1.2pt\vrule width=0.6pt,height=12.0pt}}$}}}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\;\Psi_{1}$}\hss}\kern 1.80556pt}\kern-1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 1.80556pt\hbox{\kern 1.20001pt}\kern 1.80556pt}}}\kern 11.0139pt}}\kern 66.48296pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\vbox{\hbox{\hfil}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$S\{(q_{h_{1},j}\mathbin{\vee}\overline{q}_{h_{1},j})\mathbin{\wedge}\cdots\mathbin{\wedge}(q_{h_{n},j}\mathbin{\vee}\overline{q}_{h_{n},j})\mathbin{\wedge}{\mathsf{t}}\}$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\vbox{\hbox{\hfil}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 79.30241pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.20001pt$}}\kern 79.30241pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern-1.43518pt\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 79.30241pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\{{\mathsf{s}},\equiv\}\;$}}\vbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{$\vbox to12.0pt{\hbox{\vrule width=0.6pt,height=12.0pt\kern 1.2pt\vrule width=0.6pt,height=12.0pt}}$}}}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\;\Psi_{2}$}\hss}\kern 79.30241pt}\kern-1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 79.30241pt\hbox{\kern 1.20001pt}\kern 79.30241pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}\kern 3.19444pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\vbox{\hbox{\hfil}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$S\{q_{h_{1},j}\mathbin{\vee}\cdots\mathbin{\vee}q_{h_{n},j}\mathbin{\vee}(\overline{q}_{h_{1},j}\mathbin{\wedge}\cdots\mathbin{\wedge}\overline{q}_{h_{n},j}\mathbin{\wedge}{\mathsf{t}})\}$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}

where
h_{1},\ldots,h_{n}
range over the indices of the existential
binders dependent on that
y_{j}
. It is easy to see how
\Psi_{1}
is
constructed. The construction of
\Psi_{2}
, using
{\mathsf{s}}
and
\equiv
, is standard, see,
e.g.
[
40
,
37
,
41
,
38
]
. Then, every
occurrence of
\forall y_{j}.F
is replaced by

q_{h_{1},j}\mathbin{\vee}\cdots\mathbin{\vee}q_{h_{n},j}\mathbin{\vee}(\overline{q}_{h_{1},j}\mathbin{\wedge}\cdots\mathbin{\wedge}\overline{q}_{h_{n},j}\mathbin{\wedge}F)
in the derivation
below that
\forall
-instance. Now, observe that all instances of
the
\exists
-rule introducing
x_{i}
dependent on
y_{j}
must occur
below in the derivation (otherwise
\Phi
would not be rectified).
Now consider such an instance

S\{B[x_{i}/t]\}

\exists\;

S\{\exists x_{i}.B\}

. Its context
S\{\cdot\}

must contain all the
\forall y_{j}
the
\exists x_{i}
depends on,
such that
B
is in their scope. Following the translation of the

\forall
rules above, we can therefore translate the
\exists
-rule
instance by the following derivation

S_{0}\{\overline{q}_{i,k_{1}}\mathbin{\wedge}S_{1}\{\overline{q}_{i,k_{2}}\mathbin{\wedge}\cdots S_{l-1}\{\overline{q}_{i,k_{l}}\mathbin{\wedge}S_{l}\{B^{\prime}\}\}\cdots\}\}

\scriptstyle\{{\mathsf{s}},\equiv\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Psi_{3}

S_{0}\{S_{1}\{\cdots S_{l-1}\{S_{l}\{\overline{q}_{i,k_{1}}\mathbin{\wedge}\overline{q}_{i,k_{2}}\mathbin{\wedge}\cdots q_{i,k_{l}}\mathbin{\wedge}B^{\prime}\}\}\cdots\}\}

where
k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}
are the indices of the universal binders
on which that
x_{i}
depends, and
B^{\prime}
is
B
in which all predicates
are replaced by a nullary one (step 3 in the frame construction). The
derivation
\Psi_{3}
can be constructed in the same way as

\Psi_{2}
.

Doing this to all instances of the rules
\forall
and
\exists
in

\Phi
yields indeed a propositional derivation
\Phi^{\star}

with

\llbracket\Phi^{\star}\rrbracket=\llbracket\Phi\rrbracket^{\star}
. It has
been shown by Retoré
[
42
]
and
rediscovered by Straßburger
[
38
]
that

\llbracket\Phi^{\star}\rrbracket=\langle\llbracket C^{\star}\rrbracket,\sim_{\Phi^{\star}}\rangle
cannot contain an induced
bimatching. By Lemma
34
,
\llbracket\Phi\rrbracket
does not
have an induced bimatching either. Furthermore, it follows from the
definition of
\delta_{\Phi}
that it is a dualizer for

\llbracket\Phi\rrbracket
.
∎

Remark 35
.

There is an alternative path of proving Theorem
33

by translating
\Phi
to an
\mathsf{MLL1^{X}}
-proof
\Pi
, observing that
this process preserves the linking and the dualizer. However, for
this, we have to extend the construction from the previous subsection to the
\mathsf{cut}
-rule,
and then show that linking and dualizer of a sequent proof
\Pi
are
invariant under cut elimination. This can be done similarly to
unification nets in
[
39
]
.

VIII-C

From Fonets to
\mathsf{MLL1^{X}}
Proofs

Now we are going to show how from a given fonet

\langle\mathcal{C},\sim_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle
we can construct a sequent proof
\Pi
in

\mathsf{MLL1^{X}}
such that
\llbracket\Pi\rrbracket=\langle\mathcal{C},\sim_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle
. In the
proof net literature, this operation is also called

sequentialization
. The basic idea behind our sequentialization
is to use the frame of
\mathcal{C}
, to which we can apply the
splitting tensor
theorem
, and then reconstruct the sequent proof
\Pi
.

Let
\Gamma
be a propositional sequent and
\sim_{\Gamma}
be a
linking for
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket
. A conjunction formula
A\mathbin{\wedge}B
is

splitting
or a
splitting tensor
if

\Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime},A\mathbin{\wedge}B,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
and

\mathord{\sim_{\Gamma}}=\mathord{\sim_{1}}\cup\sim_{2}
, such
that
\sim_{1}
is a linking for
\llbracket\Gamma^{\prime},A\rrbracket
and

\sim_{2}
is a linking for
\llbracket B,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket
, i.e., removing
the
\mathbin{\wedge}
from
A\mathbin{\wedge}B
splits the linked fograph
\langle\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\sim_{\Gamma}\rangle
into two fographs.
We say that
\langle\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\sim_{\Gamma}\rangle
is
mixed
iff

\Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
and

\mathord{\sim_{\Gamma}}=\mathord{\sim_{1}}\cup\sim_{2}
, such
that
\sim_{1}
is a linking for
\llbracket\Gamma^{\prime}\rrbracket
and

\sim_{2}
is a linking for
\llbracket\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket
.
Finally,
\langle\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\sim_{\Gamma}\rangle
is
splittable
if it is mixed or has a splitting tensor.

Theorem 36
.

Let
\Gamma
be a
{\mathsf{f}}
-free propositional sequent containing only atoms
and
\mathbin{\wedge}
-formulas, and
\sim_{\Gamma}
be a
linking for
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket
. If
\langle\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\sim_{\Gamma}\rangle
does not induce a bimatching then it is splittable.

This is the well-known splitting-tensor-theorem

[
19
,
43
]
, adapted for the presence of

\mathsf{mix}
. In the setting of linked cographs, it has first been proved by
Retoré
[
44
,
45
]
and then rediscovered by Hughes
[
9
]
.
We use it now for our sequentialization:

Theorem 37
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{C},\sim_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle
be a fonet, and let
\Gamma
be a sequent with
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket=\mathcal{C}
. Then there is an

\mathsf{MLL1^{X}}
-proof
\Pi
of
\Gamma
, such that

\llbracket\Pi\rrbracket=\langle\mathcal{C},\sim_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle
.

Proof.

Let
\delta_{\mathcal{C}}
be the dualizer of
\langle\mathcal{C},\sim_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle
. We
proceed by induction on the size of
\Gamma
(i.e., the number of
symbols in it, without counting the commas). If
\Gamma
contains a
formula with
\mathbin{\vee}
-root, or a formula
\forall x.A
, we can
immediately apply the
\mathbin{\vee}
-rule or the
\forall
-rule of
\mathsf{MLL1^{X}}

and proceed by induction hypothesis. If
\Gamma
contains a formula

\exists x.A
such that the corresponding binder
\bullet x
in
\mathcal{C}

has no dependency, then we can apply the
\exists
-rule, choosing the
term
t
as determined by
\delta_{\mathcal{C}}
, and proceed by induction
hypothesis. Hence, we can now assume that
\Gamma
contains only
atoms,
\mathbin{\wedge}
-formulas, or formulas of shape
\exists x.A
, where
the vertex
\bullet x
has dependencies. Then the frame

\langle\llbracket\Gamma^{\star}\rrbracket,\sim_{\Gamma^{\star}}\rangle
does
not induce a bimatching and contains only atoms and

\mathbin{\wedge}
-formulas, and is therefore splittable. If it is mixed, then
we can apply the
\mathsf{mix}
-rule to
\Gamma
and apply the induction
hypothesis to the two components. If it is not mixed then there must
be a splitting tensor. If the splitting
\mathbin{\wedge}
is already in

\Gamma
, then we can apply the
\mathbin{\wedge}
-rule and proceed by
induction hypothesis on the two branches. However, if

\Gamma^{\star}
is not mixed and all splitting tensors are

\mathbin{\wedge}
-formulas introduced in step 1 of the frame construction,
then we get a contradiction as in that case there must be a
\mathbin{\vee}
- or
\forall
-formula in
\Gamma
.
∎

VIII-D

From Fonets to
\mathsf{MLS1^{X}}
Proofs

We can now straightforwardly obtain the same result for
\mathsf{MLS1^{X}}
:

Theorem 38
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{C},\sim_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle
be a fonet, and let
C
be a formula with
\llbracket C\rrbracket=\mathcal{C}
. Then there is
a derivation

{\mathsf{t}}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{MLS1^{X}}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi

C

such that
\llbracket\Phi\rrbracket=\langle\mathcal{C},\sim_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle
.

Proof.

We apply Theorem
37
to obtain a sequent
proof
\Pi
of
\vdash C
with

\llbracket\Pi\rrbracket=\langle\mathcal{C},\sim_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle
. Then we apply
Lemma
20
, observing that the translation from

\mathsf{MLL1^{X}}
to
\mathsf{MLS1^{X}}
preserves linking and dualizer.
∎

Remark 39
.

Note that it is also possible to do a direct “sequentialization”
into the deep inference system
\mathsf{MLS1^{X}}
, using the techniques
presented in
[
38
]
and
[
46
]
.

IX
Skew Bifibrations and Resource Management

In this section we establish the relation between skew bifibrations and derivations in

\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}},{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}},\equiv\}
. However,
if a derivation
\Phi
contains instances of the rules
\mathsf{c_{\forall}}
,
\mathsf{m_{\forall}}
, and

\mathsf{m_{\exists}}
we can no longer naively define the rectification

\widehat{\Phi}
as in the previous section for
\mathsf{MLS1^{X}}
, as these two
rules cannot be applied if premise and conclusion are rectified. For
this reason we define here rectified versions
\widehat{\mathsf{c_{\forall}}}
,
\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\forall}}}
and

\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\exists}}}
, shown below:

{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 8.33328pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$S\{\forall y.\forall x.Ax\}$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\widehat{\mathsf{c_{\forall}}}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to64.23372pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 8.4641pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$S\{\forall x.Ax\}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 8.4641pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\hskip 30.00005pt\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 8.33328pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$S\{(\forall y.Ay)\mathbin{\vee}(\forall z.Bz)\}$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\forall}}}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to98.55447pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 11.50119pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$S\{\forall x.(Ax\mathbin{\vee}Bx)\}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 11.50119pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\\
\\[-4.30554pt]
{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 8.33328pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$S\{(\exists y.Ay)\mathbin{\vee}(\exists z.Bz)\}$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\exists}}}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to98.55447pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 11.50119pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$S\{\exists x.(Ax\mathbin{\vee}Bx)\}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 11.50119pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\end{array}

Here, we use the notation
A\cdot
for a formula
A
with occurrences
of a placeholder
\cdot
for a variable. Then
Ax
stands for the
results of replacing that placeholder with
x
, and also indicating
that
x
must not occur in
A\cdot
. Then
\forall x.Ax
and
\forall y.Ay
are the same formula modulo renaming of the bound variable bound
by the outermost
\forall
-quantifier. We also demand that the variables
x
,
y
, and
z
do
not occur in the context
S\{\cdot\}
.

Note that in an instance of
\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\forall}}}
or
\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\exists}}}
(as shown
above), we can have
x=y
or
x=z
, but not both if the premise is
rectified. If
x=y
and
x=z
we have
\mathsf{m_{\forall}}
and

\mathsf{m_{\exists}}
as special cases of
\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\forall}}}
and
\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\exists}}}
, respectively. And similarly, if
x=y
then
\mathsf{c_{\forall}}

is a special case of
\widehat{\mathsf{c_{\forall}}}
.

For a derivation
\Phi
in

\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}},{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}},\equiv\}
, we can now construct the

rectification

\widehat{\Phi}
by rectifying each line of
\Phi
, yielding
a derivation in

\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\mathsf{ac},\widehat{\mathsf{c_{\forall}}},{\mathsf{m}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\forall}}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\exists}}},\equiv\}
.

For each instance

Q

\mathsf{r}\;

P

of an inference rule in

\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\mathsf{ac},\widehat{\mathsf{c_{\forall}}},{\mathsf{m}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\forall}}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\exists}}},\equiv\}
we can
define the
induced map

\lfloor{\mathsf{r}}\rfloor\colon V_{\llbracket Q\rrbracket}\to V_{\llbracket P\rrbracket}
which acts as the identity for

\mathsf{r}\in\{{\mathsf{m}},\equiv\}
and as the canonical injection for

\mathsf{r}\in\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}}\}
. For
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{ac}
it maps the vertices
corresponding to the two atoms in the premise to the vertex of the
contracted atom in the conclusion, and for

\mathsf{r}\in\{\widehat{\mathsf{c_{\forall}}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\forall}}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\exists}}}\}
it maps the two vertices
corresponding to the quantifiers in the premise to the one in the
conclusion (and acts as the identity on all other vertices).
For a
derivation
\Phi
in

\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\mathsf{ac},\widehat{\mathsf{c_{\forall}}},{\mathsf{m}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\forall}}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\exists}}},\equiv\}
we can then define
the
induced map

\lfloor{\Phi}\rfloor
as the composition of the induced
maps of the rule instances in
\Phi
.

Lemma 40
.

Let

A

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}},{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}},\equiv\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi

B

be given.
Then there is a rectified derivation

\widehat{A}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\mathsf{ac},\widehat{\mathsf{c_{\forall}}},{\mathsf{m}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\forall}}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\exists}}},\equiv\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\widehat{\Phi}

\widehat{B}

, such that the induced maps

\lfloor{\Phi}\rfloor\colon\llbracket A\rrbracket\to\llbracket B\rrbracket
and

\lfloor{\widehat{\Phi}}\rfloor\colon\llbracket\widehat{A}\rrbracket\to\llbracket\widehat{B}\rrbracket
are equal up to a variable renaming of the vertex labels.

Proof.

Immediate from the definition.
∎

IX-A

From Contraction and Weakening to Skew Bifibrations

Lemma 41
.

Let

A

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\mathsf{ac},\widehat{\mathsf{c_{\forall}}},{\mathsf{m}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\forall}}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\exists}}},\equiv\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi

B

be a rectified derivation. Then the induced map

\lfloor{\Phi}\rfloor\colon\llbracket A\rrbracket\to\llbracket B\rrbracket
is a skew bifibration.

Before we show the proof of this lemma, we introduce another useful
concept: the
propositional encoding

A^{\circ}
of a formula
A
,
which is a propositional formula with the property that
\llbracket A^{\circ}\rrbracket=\llbracket A\rrbracket
. For this, we introduce new propositional variables
that have the same names as the (first-order) variables

x\in\textsc{var}
. Then
A^{\circ}
is defined inductively by:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}l}a^{\circ}&a\\
(A\vee B)^{\circ}&A^{\circ}\vee B^{\circ}\\
(A\wedge B)^{\circ}&A^{\circ}\wedge B^{\circ}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}l}(\forall xA)^{\circ}&x\vee A^{\circ}\\
(\exists xA)^{\circ}&x\wedge A^{\circ}\end{array}

Lemma 42
.

For every formula
A
, we have
\llbracket A^{\circ}\rrbracket=\llbracket A\rrbracket
.

Proof.

A straightforward induction on
A
.
∎

We use
\equiv^{\circ}
to denote the restriction of
\equiv
to
propositional formulas, i.e., the first two lines in (
2
).

Proof of Lemma
41
.

First, observe that for every inference rule

\mathsf{r}\in\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\mathsf{ac},\widehat{\mathsf{c_{\forall}}},{\mathsf{m}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\forall}}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\exists}}},\equiv\}

the induced map
\lfloor{\mathsf{r}}\rfloor\colon V_{\llbracket Q\rrbracket}\to V_{\llbracket P\rrbracket}
defines
an existential-preserving graph homomorphism
\llbracket Q\rrbracket\to\llbracket P\rrbracket
and a fibration on the corresponding binding graphs. Therefore,
their composition
\lfloor{\Phi}\rfloor
has the same properties of fibration.

For showing that it is also a skew fibration, we construct for

\Phi
its propositional encoding
\Phi^{\circ}
by
translating every line into its propositional encoding.
The instances
of the rules
\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\forall}}}
and
\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\exists}}}
are replaced by:

S\{(y\mathbin{\vee}(Ay)^{\circ})\mathbin{\vee}(z\mathbin{\vee}(Bz)^{\circ})\}

\equiv\;

S\{(y\mathbin{\vee}z)\mathbin{\vee}((Ay)^{\circ}\mathbin{\vee}(Bz)^{\circ})\}

\widehat{\mathsf{ac}}\;

S\{x\mathbin{\vee}((Ax)^{\circ}\mathbin{\vee}(Bx)^{\circ})\}

S\{(y\mathbin{\wedge}(Ay)^{\circ})\mathbin{\vee}(z\mathbin{\wedge}(Bz)^{\circ})\}

{\mathsf{m}}\;

S\{(y\mathbin{\vee}z)\mathbin{\wedge}((Ay)^{\circ}\mathbin{\vee}(Bz)^{\circ})\}

\widehat{\mathsf{ac}}\;

S\{x\mathbin{\wedge}((Ax)^{\circ}\mathbin{\vee}(Bx)^{\circ})\}

respectively, where
\widehat{\mathsf{ac}}
is a
\mathsf{ac}
that renames the
variables—the propositional variable, as well as the first-order
variable of the same name—as everything is rectified, there is no
ambiguity here. Any instance of a rule
\mathsf{w}
,
\mathsf{ac}
,
{\mathsf{m}}
, or

\equiv
is translated to an instance of the same rule,

\widehat{\mathsf{c_{\forall}}}
is translated to
\widehat{\mathsf{ac}}
, and
\mathsf{w_{\forall}}
is
translated to
\mathsf{w}
.

This gives us a derivation

A^{\circ}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{ac},\widehat{\mathsf{ac}},{\mathsf{m}},\equiv^{\circ}\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi^{\circ}

B^{\circ}

such that
\lfloor{\Phi^{\circ}}\rfloor=\lfloor{\Phi}\rfloor
. It has
been shown in
[
23
]
that
\lfloor{\Phi^{\circ}}\rfloor
is a skew
fibration.
Hence,

\lfloor{\Phi}\rfloor
is a skew fibration.
∎

IX-B

From Skew Bifibrations to Contraction and Weakening

Lemma 43
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
be fographs, let
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a skew
bifibration, and let
A
and
B
be formulas with
\llbracket A\rrbracket=\mathcal{A}

and
\llbracket B\rrbracket=\mathcal{B}
. Then there are derivations

A

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\mathsf{ac},\widehat{\mathsf{c_{\forall}}},{\mathsf{m}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\forall}}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\exists}}},\equiv\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\widehat{\Phi}

B

and

A\rho_{\varphi}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}},{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}},\equiv\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi

B

such that
\lfloor{\widehat{\Phi}}\rfloor=\varphi
and
\widehat{\Phi}
is a
rectification of
\Phi
, and
\rho_{\varphi}
is the substitution
induced by
\varphi
.

In the proof of this lemma, we make use of the following concept: Let

P

\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Psi

Q

be a derivation where
P
and
Q
are
propositional formulas (possibly using variable
x\in\textsc{var}
at the
places of atoms). We say that
\Psi
can be
lifted
to

\mathsf{S}^{\prime}
if there are (first-order) formulas
C
and
D
such that
P=C^{\circ}
and

Q=D^{\circ}
and there is a derivation

C

\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}^{\prime}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Psi^{\prime}

D

.

We say a fograph homomorphism
\varphi\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
is
full
if
for all
v,w\in V_{{\mathcal{G}}}
, we have that
\varphi(v)\varphi(w)\in E_{\mathcal{H}}
implies

vw\in E_{\mathcal{G}}
.

Lemma 44
.

Let
\varphi\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
be full and injective skew bifibration
such that
\rho_{\varphi}
is the identity substitution, and let
G

and
H
be formulas with
\llbracket G\rrbracket=\mathcal{G}
and
\llbracket H\rrbracket=\mathcal{H}
. Then
there is a derivation

G

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\equiv\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi

H

.

Proof.

By
[
23
, Proposition 7.6.1]
, we have a derivation

G^{\circ}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{w},\equiv^{\circ}\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Psi

H^{\circ}

. In order to
lift
\Psi
, we need to reorganize the instances of

\mathsf{w}
. If
H
contains a subformula
\forall x.A
which is not
present in
G
, the
\mathsf{w}
-instances in
\Psi
could introduce the
parts of the propositional encoding
x\mathbin{\vee}A
independently. We
say that an instance
\mathsf{r}_{1}
of
\mathsf{w}
in
\Phi
is
in the
scope
of an instance
\mathsf{r}_{2}
of
\mathsf{w}
if
\mathsf{r}_{1}
introduced
formulas that contain a free variable
x
(i.e.,
x
occurs in a
term in a predicate) and
\mathsf{r}_{2}
introduces the atom
x
as a
subformula (i.e. the propositional encoding of the binder
x
). We
can now permute the
\mathsf{w}
-instances in
\Psi
such that whenever
a rule instance
\mathsf{r}_{1}
is in the scope of an instance
\mathsf{r}_{2}
, then

\mathsf{r}_{2}
occurs below
\mathsf{r}_{1}
in
\Psi
.
Then we can lift
\Psi
stepwise. First, observe that each line of

\Psi
is
\equiv^{\circ}
-equivalent to the propositional encoding
P^{\circ}
of a
first-order formula
P
. We now have to show
that each instance of
\mathsf{w}
in
\Psi
is indeed the image of a correct
application of
\mathsf{w}
or
\mathsf{w_{\forall}}
in first-order logic.
If we have a
\mathsf{w}
of the form

S^{\circ}\{A^{\circ}\}

\mathsf{w}\;

S^{\circ}\{x\mathbin{\vee}A^{\circ}\}

or

S^{\circ}\{A^{\circ}\}

\mathsf{w}\;

S^{\circ}\{(x\mathbin{\vee}B^{\circ})\mathbin{\vee}A^{\circ}\}

then
x
cannot occur freely in
A
, as otherwise the fibration
property would be violated. We can therefore lift these instances to

S\{A\}

\mathsf{w_{\forall}}\;

S\{\forall x.A\}

or

S\{A\}

\mathsf{w}\;

S\{(\forall x.B)\mathbin{\vee}A\}

respectively.
If a weakening happens inside a subformula
x\mathbin{\vee}C^{\circ}
or
x\mathbin{\wedge}C^{\circ}
in
\Psi
, then there are the following cases:

S^{\circ}\{x\mathbin{\vee}C^{\circ}\}

\mathsf{w}\;

S^{\circ}\{x\mathbin{\vee}D^{\circ}\mathbin{\vee}C^{\circ}\}

S^{\circ}\{x\mathbin{\wedge}C^{\circ}\}

\mathsf{w}\;

S^{\circ}\{x\mathbin{\wedge}(D^{\circ}\mathbin{\vee}C^{\circ})\}

S^{\circ}\{x\mathbin{\wedge}C^{\circ}\}

\mathsf{w}\;

S^{\circ}\{(x\mathbin{\vee}D^{\circ})\mathbin{\wedge}C^{\circ}\}

The first two cases can be lifted to

S\{\forall x.C\}

\mathsf{w}\;

S\{\forall x.(D\mathbin{\vee}C)\}

and

S\{\exists x.C\}

\mathsf{w}\;

S\{\exists x.(D\mathbin{\vee}C)\}

respectively. But in the third case, an
\exists
-quantifier would be
transformed into an
\forall
-quantifier. But as
\varphi
has to
preserve existentials, this third case cannot occur. All other situations can be lifted trivially, giving us

G

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\equiv\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi

H

as desired.
∎

Lemma 45
.

Let
\varphi\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
be a surjective skew
bifibration, and let
G
and
H
be formulas with
\llbracket G\rrbracket=\mathcal{G}

and
\llbracket H\rrbracket=\mathcal{H}
. Then there is a derivation

G\rho_{\varphi}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}},{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}},\equiv\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi

H

where
\rho_{\varphi}
is the substitution
induced by
\varphi
.

Proof.

By
[
47
, Proposition 7.5]
, there is a derivation

(G\sigma_{\!\varphi})^{\circ}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{ac},{\mathsf{m}},\equiv^{\circ}\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Psi

H^{\circ}

. We can lift
\Psi
to a first-order
derivation in
\{\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}},{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}},\equiv\}
, in a similar
way as in the previous lemma.
The technical details are in
Appendix
-C
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
43
.

Let
V_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\prime}\subseteq V_{{\mathcal{B}}}
be the image of
\varphi
, and let
\mathcal{B}_{1}
be
the subgraph of
\mathcal{B}
induced by
V_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\prime}
. Hence, we have two maps

\varphi^{\prime\prime}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}_{1}
being a surjection and
\varphi^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{B}_{1}\to\mathcal{B}
being a full injection. Both,
\varphi^{\prime}
and
\varphi^{\prime\prime}

remain skew bifibrations. Furthermore,
\mathcal{B}_{1}
is also a fograph.
Let
B_{1}
be a formula with
\llbracket B_{1}\rrbracket=\mathcal{B}_{1}
.
We can apply Lemmas
44

and
45
to obtain derivations

B_{1}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\equiv\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi^{\prime}

B

and

A\rho_{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}},{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}},\equiv\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi^{\prime\prime}

B_{1}

As
\rho_{\varphi^{\prime}}
is the identity, we have

\rho_{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}=\rho_{\varphi}
. Hence, the composition of

\Phi^{\prime\prime}
and
\Phi^{\prime}
is the desired derivation
\Phi
. Then

\widehat{\Phi}
can be constructed by rectifying
\Phi
, where the
variables to be used in
A
are already given. That

\varphi=\lfloor{\widehat{\Phi}}\rfloor
follows immediately from the
construction.
∎

X
Summary and Proof of Main Result

The only theorem of Section
VI
that has not yet been
proved is Theorem
19
establishing the full correspondence
between decomposed proofs in
\mathsf{KS1}
and combinatorial proofs. We show
the proof here, by summarizing the results of the previous two
Sections
VIII
and
IX
.

Proof of Theorem
19
.

First, assume we have a combinatorial proof
\varphi\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{A}

and a formula
A
with
\mathcal{A}=\llbracket A\rrbracket
.
Let
C
be a formula with
\llbracket C\rrbracket=\mathcal{C}
, and let
\rho_{\varphi}

be the substitution induced by
\varphi
.
By Lemma
43
there is a derivation

C\rho_{\varphi}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}},{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}},\equiv\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi_{2}

A

Since
\mathcal{C}
is a fonet, we have by Theorem
38
a derivation

{\mathsf{t}}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{MLS1^{X}}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi^{\prime}_{1}

C

This derivation remains valid if we apply the substitution

\rho_{\varphi}
to every line in
\Phi^{\prime}_{1}
, yielding the derivation

\Phi_{1}
of
C\rho_{\varphi}
as desired.

Conversely, assume we have a decomposed derivation

{\mathsf{t}}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{MLS1^{X}}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi_{1}

A^{\prime}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}},{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}},\equiv\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi_{2}

A

(6)

Then we can transform
\Phi_{1}
into a rectified form

\widehat{\Phi}_{1}
, proving
\widehat{A}^{\prime}
. By
Theorem
33
, the linked fograph

\llbracket\widehat{\Phi}_{1}\rrbracket=\langle\llbracket\widehat{A}^{\prime}\rrbracket,\sim_{\widehat{\Phi}_{1}}\rangle
is a fonet. Then, by
Lemma
40
, there is a rectified derivation

\widehat{A^{\prime}}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\mathsf{ac},\widehat{\mathsf{c_{\forall}}},{\mathsf{m}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\forall}}},\widehat{\mathsf{m_{\exists}}},\equiv\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\widehat{\Phi_{2}}

\widehat{A}

whose induced map

\lfloor{\widehat{\Phi_{2}}}\rfloor\colon\llbracket\widehat{A^{\prime}}\rrbracket\to\llbracket\widehat{A}\rrbracket
is the same as the induced map

\lfloor{\Phi_{2}}\rfloor\colon\llbracket A^{\prime}\rrbracket\to\llbracket A\rrbracket
of
\Phi_{2}
. By
Lemma
41
, this map is a skew bifibration. Hence, we
have a combinatorial proof
\varphi\colon\mathcal{C}\to\llbracket A\rrbracket
with

\mathcal{C}=\llbracket\widehat{A^{\prime}}\rrbracket
.
∎

Note that Theorem
19
shows at the same time soundness, completeness, and full completeness, as

1.

every proof in
\mathsf{KS1}
can be translated into a combinatorial proof, and

2.

every combinatorial proof is the image of a
\mathsf{KS1}
-proof under that translation.

XI
Conclusion

We uncovered a close correspondence between
first-order combinatorial proofs and decomposed deep inference
derivations of system
\mathsf{KS1}
, and showed that every proof in

\mathsf{KS1}
has such a decomposed form.

The most surprising discovery for us was that all technical
difficulties in our work could be reduced (in a non-trivial way) to
the propositional setting.

The obvious next step in our research is to investigate proof
composition and normalisation of first-order combinatorial
proofs. Even in the propositional setting, the normalisation of
combinatorial proofs is underdeveloped. There exist two different
procedures for cut elimination for combinatorial proofs in classical
propositional logic
[
10
,
12
]
, but both have
their insufficiencies, and have not been extended to other
logics.

We hope to garner new insights on the normalisation of
classical first-order proofs through our work on combinatorial proofs.

-A

Proof of Theorem
29

Proof of Theorem
29
.

Write
\textsf{\small fv}(A)
for the set of variables which occur free in
A
.

Note that the instances of
\mathsf{w},\mathsf{c}
in
\Phi_{2}
are deep, but inside sequent contexts.

First, if an instance of

\vdash\Gamma

\mathsf{wk}\;

\vdash\Gamma,A

is followed by a rule in which
A
is not in the principal
formula, it can be permuted downwards.
Otherwise, the proof can be transformed using the following rewriting rules.

Note that in the case of
\mathbin{\vee}
, we use the deep rule
\mathsf{w}
which can be
permuted under all the rules. By using these rewriting rules, we can eventually get
a derivation with all the instances of
\mathsf{wk}
and
\mathsf{w}
at the bottom. Now observe
that the instances of
\mathsf{ctr}
in
\Phi
can be transformed using the following
rule:

{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 14.24995pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\vdash\Gamma,A,A\phantom{\vdash}$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{ctr}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to45.13881pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 5.97221pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\Gamma,A\phantom{\vdash}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 5.97221pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\leadsto{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 9.4444pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 1.11113pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\vdash\Gamma,A,A\phantom{\vdash}$}}}\kern 1.11113pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathbin{\vee}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to47.36108pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\Gamma,A\mathbin{\vee}A\phantom{\vdash}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 9.4444pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{c}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to47.36108pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 16.52774pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\Gamma,A\phantom{\vdash}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 7.08334pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}

Knowing that
\mathsf{c}
can be permuted under all the rules of
\mathsf{MLL1^{X}}
, we
eventually obtain a derivation:

\scriptstyle\mathsf{MLL1^{X}}\kern 2.5pt

\kern 2.5pt\scriptstyle\Phi^{\prime}_{1}

\vdash\Gamma^{\prime}\phantom{\vdash}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{wk},\mathsf{w},\mathsf{c},\equiv\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi^{\prime}_{2}

\vdash\Gamma\phantom{\vdash}

Note that
\equiv
is required here since the permutation of formulas is implicit
in
\mathsf{MLL1^{X}}
.

By transforming each sequent of
\Phi^{\prime}_{2}
into its corresponding formula, and by
considering the following rewriting rule:

{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 14.49998pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 5.97221pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\vdash\Gamma\phantom{\vdash}$}}}\kern 5.97221pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{wk}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to33.19438pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\Gamma,A\phantom{\vdash}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\leadsto{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 9.61107pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 7.08334pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\vdash\bigvee\mkern-1.0mu(\mkern-2.0mu\Gamma\mkern-2.0mu)\phantom{\vdash}$}}}\kern 7.08334pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{w}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to48.7501pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\bigvee\mkern-1.0mu(\mkern-2.0mu\Gamma\mkern-2.0mu)\mathbin{\vee}A\phantom{\vdash}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}

-B

Rule permutation for the proof of Lemma
31

We construct a rewriting system based on rule permutation on derivations in
\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}},{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}},\equiv\}
that allows us to reach a derivation of the
form

A

\scriptstyle\{{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}},\equiv\}\;

A^{\prime}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}}\}\;

B^{\prime}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}},\equiv\}\;

B

from any derivation. Intuitively, we want to move all the instances of
\mathsf{r}\in\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}}\}
downwards and all the instances of
\mathsf{r}^{\prime}\in\{{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}}\}
upwards.

We first study the interactions between two rules. Certain cases are unsolved at
this stage, and they are considered later when we study the interactions between
two non-
\equiv
rule instances separated by
\equiv
. Only non-trivial cases are
presented here:

•

\mathsf{r}_{1}/\mathsf{r}_{2}
, where
\mathsf{r}_{1}\in\{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w_{\forall}}\}
and
\mathsf{r}_{2}\in\{\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}},{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}}\}
:

{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 2.41666pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 9.61107pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 5.97629pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$a$}}}\kern 5.97629pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{w}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to17.23846pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$a\mathbin{\vee}a$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 9.61107pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{ac}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to17.23846pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 15.58736pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 2.35706pt\hbox{$a$}\kern 2.35706pt$}}\kern 5.97629pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\leadsto{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{$a$}}}}

{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 1.1111pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 9.61107pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 22.76636pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$A\mathbin{\wedge}C$}}}\kern 22.76636pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{w}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to67.56192pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$(A\mathbin{\wedge}C)\mathbin{\vee}(B\mathbin{\wedge}D)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 9.61107pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{${\mathsf{m}}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to67.56192pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 9.61107pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$(A\mathbin{\vee}B)\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\leadsto{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 9.61107pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 1.88965pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 9.61107pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 11.26564pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$A\mathbin{\wedge}C$}}}\kern 11.26564pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{w}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to44.56049pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$(A\mathbin{\vee}B)\mathbin{\wedge}C$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}\kern 11.50072pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{w}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to67.56192pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$(A\mathbin{\vee}B)\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}

{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 1.1111pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 14.63885pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 12.57983pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$A\mathbin{\vee}(\forall x.B)$}}}\kern 12.57983pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{w_{\forall}}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to72.57288pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$(\forall x.A)\mathbin{\vee}(\forall x.B)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 14.63885pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{m_{\forall}}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to72.57288pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 27.21869pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\forall x.(A\mathbin{\vee}B)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 12.57983pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\leadsto{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 10.55551pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$A\mathbin{\vee}(\forall x.B)$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\equiv\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to47.4132pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\forall x.(A\mathbin{\vee}B)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}

where in the last case,

.

•

\mathsf{r}_{1}/\mathsf{r}_{2}
, where
\mathsf{r}_{1}\in\{\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}}\}
and

\mathsf{w}/\equiv
:

A

\mathsf{w}\;

A\mathbin{\vee}B

\equiv\;

B\mathbin{\vee}A

A\mathbin{\vee}C

\mathsf{w}\;

(A\mathbin{\vee}B)\mathbin{\vee}C)

\equiv\;

A\mathbin{\vee}(B\mathbin{\vee}C)

In the following two cases, we assume
x\neq y
(otherwise they are trivial).

\equiv/\mathsf{c_{\forall}}
:

\forall x.\forall y.\forall x.A

\equiv\;

\forall x.\forall x.\forall y.A

\mathsf{c_{\forall}}\;

\forall x.\forall y.A

\forall x.\forall y.\forall x.A

\equiv\;

\forall y.\forall x.\forall x.A

\mathsf{c_{\forall}}\;

\forall y.\forall x.A

\forall x.((\forall x.A)\mathbin{\vee}B)

\equiv\;

\;(x\notin\textsf{\small fv}(B))

(\forall x.\forall x.A)\mathbin{\vee}B

\mathsf{c_{\forall}}\;

(\forall x.A)\mathbin{\vee}B

\forall x.((\forall x.A)\mathbin{\vee}B)

\equiv\;

\;(x\notin\textsf{\small fv}(B))

\forall x.\forall x.(A\mathbin{\vee}B)

\mathsf{c_{\forall}}\;

\forall x.(A\mathbin{\vee}B)

\equiv/{\mathsf{m}}
:

(C\mathbin{\wedge}A)\mathbin{\vee}(B\mathbin{\wedge}D)

\equiv\;

(A\mathbin{\wedge}C)\mathbin{\vee}(B\mathbin{\wedge}D)

{\mathsf{m}}\;

(A\mathbin{\vee}B)\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D)

{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.16666pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 10.55551pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$(B\mathbin{\wedge}D)\mathbin{\vee}(A\mathbin{\wedge}C)$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\equiv\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to67.56192pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$(A\mathbin{\wedge}C)\mathbin{\vee}(B\mathbin{\wedge}D)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 10.55551pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{${\mathsf{m}}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to67.56192pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 10.55551pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$(A\mathbin{\vee}B)\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\leadsto{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 10.72217pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$(B\mathbin{\wedge}D)\mathbin{\vee}(A\mathbin{\wedge}C)$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{${\mathsf{m}}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to67.56192pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$(B\mathbin{\vee}A)\mathbin{\wedge}(D\mathbin{\vee}C)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 10.72217pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\equiv\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to67.56192pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 10.72217pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$(A\mathbin{\vee}B)\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}

((A\mathbin{\wedge}C)\mathbin{\wedge}E)\mathbin{\vee}(B\mathbin{\wedge}D)

\equiv\;

(A\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\wedge}E))\mathbin{\vee}(B\mathbin{\wedge}D)

{\mathsf{m}}\;

(A\mathbin{\vee}B)\mathbin{\wedge}((C\mathbin{\wedge}E)\mathbin{\vee}D)

(\forall x.(A\mathbin{\wedge}C))\mathbin{\vee}(B\mathbin{\wedge}D)

\equiv\;

\;(x\notin\textsf{\small fv}(B\mathbin{\wedge}D))

\forall x.((A\mathbin{\wedge}C)\mathbin{\vee}(B\mathbin{\wedge}D))

{\mathsf{m}}\;

\forall x.((A\mathbin{\vee}B)\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D))

\equiv/\mathsf{m_{\forall}}
:

{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 5.19444pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 10.55551pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$(\forall x.B)\mathbin{\vee}(\forall x.A)$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\equiv\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to72.57288pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$(\forall x.A)\mathbin{\vee}(\forall x.B)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 10.55551pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{m_{\forall}}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to72.57288pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 23.13535pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\forall x.(A\mathbin{\vee}B)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 12.57983pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\leadsto{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 15.74995pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$(\forall x.B)\mathbin{\vee}(\forall x.A)$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{m_{\forall}}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to72.57288pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 12.57983pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\forall x.(B\mathbin{\vee}A)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 12.57983pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 28.32979pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\equiv\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to47.4132pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 12.57983pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 28.32979pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\forall x.(A\mathbin{\vee}B)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 12.57983pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}

(\forall y.\forall x.A)\mathbin{\vee}(\forall x.B)

\equiv\;

(\forall x.\forall y.A)\mathbin{\vee}(\forall x.B)

\mathsf{m_{\forall}}\;

\forall x.((\forall y.A)\mathbin{\vee}B)

\forall x.(A\mathbin{\vee}(\forall x.B))

\equiv\;

(\forall x.A)\mathbin{\vee}(\forall x.B)

\mathsf{m_{\forall}}\;

\forall x.(A\mathbin{\vee}B)

•

\equiv/\mathsf{m_{\exists}}
: similar to
\equiv/\mathsf{m_{\forall}}

Interactions between two non-
\equiv
rules with the presence of
\equiv
in
between:

•

\mathsf{c_{\forall}}/\equiv/\mathsf{r}
where
\mathsf{r}\in\{{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}}\}
: First permute

\mathsf{c_{\forall}}
under
\equiv
and then permute
\mathsf{c_{\forall}}
under
\mathsf{r}
.

•

\mathsf{ac}/\equiv/\mathsf{r}
where
\mathsf{r}\in\{{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}}\}
: First permute

\mathsf{ac}
under
\equiv
and then permute
\mathsf{ac}
under
\mathsf{r}
.

0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 23.13535pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\exists x.(A\mathbin{\vee}B)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 12.57983pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\leadsto{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.94444pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 9.61107pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 10.97224pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\exists x.B$}}}\kern 10.97224pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{w}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to47.4132pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\exists x.(B\mathbin{\vee}A)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 9.61107pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\equiv\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to47.4132pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 9.61107pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\exists x.(A\mathbin{\vee}B)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}

-C

Proof of Lemma
45

Proof of Lemma
45
.

By

[
47
, Proposition 7.5]
, there is a derivation

(G\rho_{\varphi})^{\circ}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{ac},{\mathsf{m}},\equiv^{\circ}\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Psi

H^{\circ}

,
We plan to show that
\Psi
can be lifted to

\{\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}},{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}},\equiv\}
. However, observe that not
every formula occurring in
\Psi
is a propositional
encoding. There are two reasons for this: (i) we might have

P\equiv^{\circ}Q
where
P
is a propositional encoding but
Q
is not,
and (ii) the rule
\mathsf{ac}
can duplicate an atom

x\in\textsc{var}
. Let us write
\mathsf{ac}_{x}
for such instances.
The problem with (i) is that we could have the following situation

S\{(x\mathbin{\wedge}(E\mathbin{\wedge}C))\mathbin{\vee}(x\mathbin{\wedge}(F\mathbin{\wedge}D))\}

\equiv^{\circ}\;

S\{((x\mathbin{\wedge}E)\mathbin{\wedge}C)\mathbin{\vee}((x\mathbin{\wedge}F)\mathbin{\wedge}D)\}

{\mathsf{m}}\;

S\{((x\mathbin{\wedge}E)\mathbin{\vee}(x\mathbin{\wedge}F))\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D)\}

(7)

where
x
occurs in
C\mathbin{\vee}D
. Then premise and conclusion are both
propositional encodings, but the whole derivation cannot be
lifted. However, since we demand that the mapping is a fibration
(and therefore a homomorphism) on the binding graphs, there must be
another instance of
{\mathsf{m}}
further below in the derivation:

S^{\prime}\{(x\mathbin{\wedge}E)\mathbin{\vee}(x\mathbin{\wedge}F)\}

{\mathsf{m}}\;

S^{\prime}\{(x\mathbin{\vee}x)\mathbin{\wedge}(E\mathbin{\vee}F)\}

(8)

We can permute both instances via the following more general scheme
(see
[
23
,
48
]
for a general discussion on
permutations of the
{\mathsf{m}}
-rule):

{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 10.72217pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 8.40904pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$S\{(G\mathbin{\wedge}E\mathbin{\wedge}C)\mathbin{\vee}(G\mathbin{\wedge}F\mathbin{\wedge}D)\}$}}}\kern 8.40904pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{${\mathsf{m}}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to130.33765pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$S\{((G\mathbin{\wedge}E)\mathbin{\vee}(H\mathbin{\wedge}F))\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D)\}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 10.72217pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{${\mathsf{m}}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to130.33765pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 14.61107pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$S\{(G\mathbin{\vee}H)\mathbin{\wedge}(E\mathbin{\vee}F)\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D)\}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 3.8889pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\;\leftrightarrow\;{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 10.72217pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 8.40904pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$S\{(G\mathbin{\wedge}E\mathbin{\wedge}C)\mathbin{\vee}(G\mathbin{\wedge}F\mathbin{\wedge}D)\}$}}}\kern 8.40904pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{${\mathsf{m}}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to130.33765pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$S\{(G\mathbin{\vee}H)\mathbin{\wedge}((E\mathbin{\wedge}C)\mathbin{\vee}(F\mathbin{\wedge}D))\}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 10.72217pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{${\mathsf{m}}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to130.33765pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 14.61107pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$S\{(G\mathbin{\vee}H)\mathbin{\wedge}(E\mathbin{\vee}F)\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D)\}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 3.8889pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}

(9)

We omitted some instances of
\equiv^{\circ}
and some parentheses. We
now call instances of
{\mathsf{m}}
as in (
7
)

illegal
, and we can transform
\Psi
through

{\mathsf{m}}
-permutations (
9
) into a derivation that
does not contain any illegal
{\mathsf{m}}
-instances.
To
address (ii),
we also apply a permutation argument, permuting all
instances of
\mathsf{ac}_{x}
up until they either reach the top of the
derivation or an instance of
{\mathsf{m}}
which separates the two atoms in
the premise. More precisely, we consider the following inference rule

S_{0}\{S_{1}\{x\}\mathbin{\vee}S_{2}\{x\}\}

\mathsf{ac}_{x}^{\equiv}\;

S\{x\}

(10)

where
S_{1}\{\cdot\}\equiv\{\cdot\}\mathbin{\vee}E
and

S_{2}\{\cdot\}\equiv\{\cdot\}\mathbin{\vee}F
and
S\{\cdot\}\equiv S_{0}\{\{\cdot\}\mathbin{\vee}E\mathbin{\vee}F\}
for some formulas
E
and
F
, where
E
or
F
or both might be empty. The
rule
\mathsf{ac}_{x}^{\equiv}
permutes over
\equiv
,
\mathsf{ac}
, and other instances of
\mathsf{ac}_{x}^{\equiv}
,
and over instances of
{\mathsf{m}}
if they occur inside
S_{0}
or
S_{1}
or

S_{2}
. The only situation in which
\mathsf{ac}_{x}^{\equiv}
cannot be permuted up is
the following:

S\{(R_{1}\{x\}\mathbin{\wedge}C)\mathbin{\vee}(R_{2}\{x\}\mathbin{\wedge}D)\}

{\mathsf{m}}\;

S\{(R_{1}\{x\}\mathbin{\vee}R_{2}\{x\})\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D)\}

\mathsf{ac}_{x}^{\equiv}\;

S\{R\{x\}\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D)\}

(11)

We can therefore assume that all instances of
\mathsf{ac}_{x}
, that contract
an atom
x\in\textsc{var}
are either at the top of
\Psi
or below a

{\mathsf{m}}
-instance as in (
11
). We now lift
\Psi
to

\{\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}},{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}},\equiv\}
, proceed by induction on
the height of
\Psi
, beginning at the top, making a case
analysis on the topmost rule that is not a
\equiv
.

•

\mathsf{ac}_{x}
: We know that the premise of (
10
) is a
propositional encoding. Hence,
S_{1}\{\cdot\}=\{\cdot\}\mathbin{\vee}E^{\circ}
and

S_{2}\{\cdot\}=\{\cdot\}\mathbin{\vee}F^{\circ}
and both
x
are universals, and

E^{\circ}\mathbin{\vee}F^{\circ}
contains all occurrences of
x
bound by that
universal. We have the following subcases:

–

E
and
F
are both non-empty: We have

S^{\circ}\{(x\mathbin{\vee}E^{\circ})\mathbin{\vee}(x\mathbin{\vee}F^{\circ})\}

\mathsf{ac}_{x}^{\equiv}\;

S^{\circ}\{x\mathbin{\vee}(E^{\circ}\mathbin{\vee}F^{\circ})\}

which can be lifted to

S\{(\forall x.E)\mathbin{\vee}(\forall x.F)\}

\mathsf{m_{\forall}}\;

S\{\forall x.(E\mathbin{\vee}F)\}

where
S^{\circ}\{\cdot\},E^{\circ},F^{\circ}
are the propositional encodings of
S\{\cdot\},E,F
, respectively.

–

E^{\circ}
is empty and
F^{\circ}
is non-empty: We have

S^{\circ}\{x\mathbin{\vee}(x\mathbin{\vee}F^{\circ})\}

\mathsf{ac}_{x}^{\equiv}\;

S^{\circ}\{x\mathbin{\vee}F^{\circ})\}

which can be lifted to

S\{\forall x.\forall x.F\}

\mathsf{c_{\forall}}\;

S\{\forall x.F\}

–

E^{\circ}
is non-empty and
F^{\circ}
is empty: This is similar to the previous case.

–

E^{\circ}
and
F^{\circ}
are both empty: This is impossible as the
premise would not be a propositional encoding.

•

\mathsf{ac}
(contracting an ordinary atom): This can trivially be lifted.

•

{\mathsf{m}}
: There are several cases to consider.

–

If none of the four principal formulas in the premise is
x

or
x\mathbin{\vee}F
for some formula
F
and
x\in\textsc{var}
, then this
instance of
{\mathsf{m}}
can trivially be lifted, and we can
proceed by induction hypothesis.

–

If exactly one of the four principal formulas in the premise
is
x
for some
x\in\textsc{var}
, then this
x
is the encoding of an existential in the
premise and of an universal in the conclusion. This is impossible,
as
\varphi
has to preserve existentials.

–

If two of the four principal formulas in the premise
are
x
for some
x\in\textsc{var}
, then we are in the following special case of (
11
):

S\{(x\mathbin{\wedge}C)\mathbin{\vee}(x\mathbin{\wedge}D)\}

{\mathsf{m}}\;

S\{(x\mathbin{\vee}x)\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D)\}

\mathsf{ac}_{x}^{\equiv}\;

S\{x\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D)\}

which can be lifted immediately to

S\{(\exists x.C)\mathbin{\vee}(\exists x.D)\}

\mathsf{m_{\exists}}\;

S\{\exists x.(C\mathbin{\vee}D)\}

–

We have a situation (
11
) where

R_{1}\{x\}\equiv x\mathbin{\vee}E
for some
E
and
R_{2}\{x\}\equiv x\mathbin{\vee}F
for some
F
with
R\{x\}\equiv x\mathbin{\vee}E\mathbin{\vee}F
(Otherwise, the application of
\mathsf{ac}_{x}^{\equiv}
would not be correct.)
That means, we have:

S\{((x\mathbin{\vee}E)\mathbin{\wedge}C)\mathbin{\vee}((x\mathbin{\vee}F)\mathbin{\wedge}D)\}

{\mathsf{m}}\;

S\{((x\mathbin{\vee}E)\mathbin{\vee}(x\mathbin{\vee}F))\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D)\}

\mathsf{ac}_{x}^{\equiv}\;

S\{(x\mathbin{\vee}E\mathbin{\vee}F)\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D)\}

which can be lifted to

S\{((\forall x.E)\mathbin{\wedge}C)\mathbin{\vee}((\forall x.F)\mathbin{\wedge}D)\}

{\mathsf{m}}\;

S\{((\forall x.E)\mathbin{\vee}(\forall x.F))\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D)\}

\mathsf{m_{\forall}}\;

S\{(\forall x.(E\mathbin{\vee}F))\mathbin{\wedge}(C\mathbin{\vee}D)\}

–

In all other cases (e.g. exactly one of the principal formulas
is of shape
x\mathbin{\vee}F
(and none is
x
), we can trivially lift
the
{\mathsf{m}}
-instance, as the quantifier structure is not affected.

Thus
\Psi
can be lifted to

G\rho_{\varphi}

\scriptstyle\{\mathsf{ac},\mathsf{c_{\forall}},{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{m_{\forall}},\mathsf{m_{\exists}},\equiv\}\;

\scriptstyle\;\Phi

H

.
∎
